Education Administration English

An education administration English curriculum for enrollment, student support, curriculum planning, parent communication, faculty coordination, compliance, accreditation, and institutional decision-making.

Words into action

Make yourself clear.

For school administrators, curriculum coordinators, student-services staff, program directors, admissions teams, registrars, and education operations leaders.

What you will practice · Choose your pace

What you will practice

  • Explain an allocation decision without disclosing other applicants or promising a future offer.
  • Align a measurable learning outcome with classroom preparation, assessment evidence, and a shared scoring rule.
  • Distinguish need, implementation, and outcome when reviewing a student-support plan.
  • Acknowledge the effect of a message, correct unsupported wording, and arrange a clear, accessible meeting.
  • Resolve scoring disagreements through shared criteria and evidence rather than averaging incompatible judgments.

Choose your pace

Quick practice · 15 minutes: complete one short exchange, check the explanations, and read both roles aloud.

Extended practice · two or three sittings: check the case facts, study the vocabulary and phrases, complete the longer conversation, then rehearse the follow-up exchange.

Level guide: designed for intermediate to advanced learners. B1 learners can start with the short exchanges and supplied phrases; B2 learners can complete the cloze conversations; C1 learners can focus on the distinctions in the language checks. These are teaching suggestions, not a certified level assessment.

Prompts

All institutions, people, figures, deadlines, and local procedures in the scenarios are fictional. Admissions, safeguarding, disability support, privacy, employment, and accreditation requirements vary by institution and jurisdiction. Use the applicable approved process and qualified advice; this book teaches communication, not legal or safeguarding decisions.

01Enrollment, Admissions, and PlacementEligible, but no place available yet+

Your goal

Explain an allocation decision without disclosing other applicants or promising a future offer.

This lesson in the book, page 4 ↗

A Read the situation

Eligible, but no place available yet

Admissions officer Leena speaks with guardian Mr. Ortiz about a fictional program with 20 places and 24 eligible applicants. The published process uses a lottery among eligible applicants. His child met the entry criteria but was not selected and is fourth on the waiting list. The local procedure allows a factual-error review requested within five school days of the decision notice. A review is not a second lottery, and no vacancy is confirmed. Leena may explain the family's own record and published process, not other applicants' information.

Leena: Admissions officer / Mr. Ortiz: Guardian

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. Why was an offer not made?

Answer and explanation

D. The eligible applicant was not selected in the capacity-limited lottery.

The brief confirms eligibility but states that the lottery did not select the applicant.

2. What is the current waiting-list position?

Answer and explanation

A. Fourth

The child's supplied waiting-list position is fourth, not the total number of applicants.

3. What does the local review procedure examine?

Answer and explanation

D. A claimed factual error in the application decision

The fictional procedure provides a factual-error review, not an automatic second allocation.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Eligibility means the applicant meets the conditions for consideration. It does not guarantee selection in an oversubscribed program. Selection, acceptance of an offer, enrollment, and class placement should not be treated as interchangeable.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
admissions
The process of considering applicants for entry.explain the admissions process
applicant
A person seeking entry to a program.contact an applicant
eligibility
Meeting the conditions for consideration.confirm eligibility
entry criteria
The published conditions an applicant must satisfy.apply entry criteria
selection
Choosing among applicants for available places.explain the selection process
capacity
The number of places available.state program capacity
oversubscribed
Having more applicants than available places.an oversubscribed program
allocation
Assignment of available places under a process.review place allocation
lottery
A random selection process among an eligible group.conduct a published lottery
offer
A formal invitation to take an available place.issue an offer
waiting list
A list of applicants awaiting possible vacancies.confirm a waiting-list position
vacancy
An available, unfilled place.confirm a vacancy
decision notice
The communication of an application outcome.send a decision notice
factual error
An incorrect statement or record of fact.identify a factual error
review request
A request to examine a decision under a stated process.submit a review request
review window
The period allowed for requesting review.explain the review window
school day
A day counted under the institution's school-day definition.count five school days
supporting document
Material supplied to substantiate a claim.attach a supporting document
enrollment
Formal registration after the applicable admission steps.complete enrollment
placement
Assignment to an appropriate class, level, or setting.discuss class placement
intake
A group admitted during a particular entry period.plan the next intake
confidential record
Information subject to restricted access or disclosure.protect confidential records
published procedure
An officially available description of the process.follow the published procedure
acknowledgment
Confirmation that a message or request was received.send an acknowledgment

The five-school-day window belongs to this fictional institution. Explain the actual deadline from the decision notice in a real case. Fourth on the waiting list is a position, not a probability or a guaranteed waiting time.

C Notice the language

Explain the rule without sounding dismissive

A family may hear a capacity decision as a judgment about the child. Acknowledge that concern, distinguish eligibility from selection, and give a precise next step. Warmth does not require predicting a waiting-list outcome or discussing another family.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Acknowledge disappointment
"I understand why this outcome is disappointing."
Confirm eligibility
"Your child met the published entry criteria."
Separate selection
"Eligibility did not guarantee a place in this intake."
Explain capacity
"There were 20 places for 24 eligible applicants."
Name the process
"The published procedure used a lottery."
State the current position
"Your child is fourth on the waiting list."
Limit a prediction
"No vacancy is confirmed at present."
Protect another family
"I cannot discuss another applicant's record."
Offer permitted information
"I can explain your child's record and the published process."
Define review scope
"The review examines a claimed factual error."
Avoid a false promise
"A review is not a second lottery."
Specify the window
"The local window is five school days from the notice."
Request evidence
"Please identify the disputed fact and supporting document."
Confirm receipt
"We will acknowledge your review request."
Distinguish later stages
"Enrollment begins only after the relevant offer steps."
Close clearly
"I will send the procedure and confirm the current waiting-list position."
6 language and precision notes
Eligible
Meets entry conditions; does not necessarily receive an offer.
Selected
Chosen through the applicable allocation process.
Waiting
Does not establish a date when a place will become available.
Review
Has a defined scope, not an unlimited reconsideration.
School days
May differ from calendar or business days.
Cannot disclose
Pair the boundary with information you can provide.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which response best corrects a misunderstanding about eligibility?

Answer and explanation

C. "Your child was eligible; the capacity-limited lottery determined the offer."

This response separates the confirmed entry conditions from the subsequent allocation decision.

2. Which request can Leena address within the supplied scope?

Answer and explanation

C. Explain the child's own decision record.

The permitted scope includes this family's record but excludes other applicants' information.

3. Which statement about the review is accurate?

Answer and explanation

C. It examines a claimed factual error under the local procedure.

The stated procedure defines review by factual error and promises no new allocation.

4. What should Leena say about timing?

Answer and explanation

A. "No vacancy is confirmed, so I cannot give an offer date."

Waiting-list position alone supplies neither a confirmed vacancy nor a reliable offer date.

E Conversations

Eligible, but no place available yet

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Leena: Admissions officer / Mr. Ortiz: Guardian

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • vacancy
  • supporting document
  • decision notice
  • factual error
  • lottery
  • waiting list
  • acknowledgment
  • Placement
  • review window
  • eligibility
  1. Mr. Ortiz
    The letter says my daughter meets the criteria, but she has no place. Did someone decide she was not suitable?
  2. Leena
    Her 1 is confirmed. The program had twenty places and twenty-four eligible applicants. Meeting the entry conditions allowed her into the allocation process, but did not guarantee selection.
  3. Mr. Ortiz
    Was this a comparison of ability, or was something missing from my daughter's file?
  4. Leena
    The published process used a 2 among eligible applicants. I can explain your daughter's record and that process, but I cannot discuss other families' applications or personal information.
  5. Mr. Ortiz
    All right, so her application passed that stage. Where is she on the list, and should we keep looking for another place?
  6. Leena
    She is fourth on the 3. No vacancy is confirmed at present. I understand that makes planning difficult, but I do not want to give you an unsupported date.
  7. Mr. Ortiz
    Does fourth mean that four children would have to leave? I do not want to misunderstand the number and make plans the school cannot support.
  8. Leena
    It states her current position, not an offer timetable. Any 4 would be handled under the published procedure. I can send that procedure so you can see how the list operates.
  9. Mr. Ortiz
    I also noticed a review option. Can I ask for the lottery to be run again because we are disappointed with the result?
  10. Leena
    The local review examines a claimed 5. It is not a second lottery. If you believe a fact in your daughter's decision record is wrong, identify it and provide supporting material.
  11. Mr. Ortiz
    I would like to check the record first. How long do I have to request that review, and where should I find the precise instructions?
  12. Leena
    The 6 is five school days from the decision notice. I will send the instructions with the record information you are authorized to receive.
  13. Mr. Ortiz
    If I identify an incorrect fact, should I send a whole new application, or just explain the issue and attach the relevant evidence?
  14. Leena
    Identify the disputed fact and attach the relevant 7. The review team needs a clear account of the issue. A new application is not what this procedure asks for.
  15. Mr. Ortiz
    If I send that document today, what will your reply actually confirm? I do not want to mistake a receipt for a changed decision.
  16. Leena
    An 8 confirms receipt, not the outcome. The review team still needs to examine the identified fact. I cannot promise that the result will change.
  17. Mr. Ortiz
    Thank you. I had also assumed the placement discussion would happen now, but perhaps that is a different stage from this application review.
  18. Leena
    Yes. 9 concerns the class or setting, rather than whether a place has been offered. We should keep those stages separate while the admissions position remains unchanged.
  19. Mr. Ortiz
    Please send the procedure, the permitted record information, and confirmation that she remains fourth. I will check for any factual error before deciding about review.
  20. Leena
    I will include the relevant 10 details. Your daughter met the entry criteria; the lottery did not select her; and no vacancy is currently confirmed. Those are the facts we can rely on.

Answers and explanations
  1. eligibility Turn 2. Eligibility confirms that the child met the entry criteria, even though the later allocation did not produce an offer.
  2. lottery Turn 4. The lottery, rather than a comparative judgment of merit, was the supplied allocation method among eligible applicants.
  3. waiting list Turn 6. The waiting list records the applicant's current position for possible vacancies, not a confirmed future offer.
  4. vacancy Turn 8. A vacancy is an actual available place; the supplied case confirms none at present.
  5. factual error Turn 10. A factual error concerns an incorrect fact or record, which is the stated scope of this review process.
  6. review window Turn 12. The review window is the locally specified period for requesting review, here five school days from the notice.
  7. supporting document Turn 14. The supporting document substantiates the particular claimed error rather than reopening every aspect of the application.
  8. acknowledgment Turn 16. An acknowledgment confirms receipt; it does not establish that the review has accepted the claim or changed the outcome.
  9. Placement Turn 18. Placement assigns a learner to a class or setting and is distinct from eligibility and the offer decision.
  10. decision notice Turn 20. The decision notice anchors the outcome and review timing, helping the family connect the instructions to the correct case.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Mr. Ortiz: The letter says my daughter meets the criteria, but she has no place. Did someone decide she was not suitable?
  2. Leena: Her eligibility is confirmed. The program had twenty places and twenty-four eligible applicants. Meeting the entry conditions allowed her into the allocation process, but did not guarantee selection.
  3. Mr. Ortiz: Was this a comparison of ability, or was something missing from my daughter's file?
  4. Leena: The published process used a lottery among eligible applicants. I can explain your daughter's record and that process, but I cannot discuss other families' applications or personal information.
  5. Mr. Ortiz: All right, so her application passed that stage. Where is she on the list, and should we keep looking for another place?
  6. Leena: She is fourth on the waiting list. No vacancy is confirmed at present. I understand that makes planning difficult, but I do not want to give you an unsupported date.
  7. Mr. Ortiz: Does fourth mean that four children would have to leave? I do not want to misunderstand the number and make plans the school cannot support.
  8. Leena: It states her current position, not an offer timetable. Any vacancy would be handled under the published procedure. I can send that procedure so you can see how the list operates.
  9. Mr. Ortiz: I also noticed a review option. Can I ask for the lottery to be run again because we are disappointed with the result?
  10. Leena: The local review examines a claimed factual error. It is not a second lottery. If you believe a fact in your daughter's decision record is wrong, identify it and provide supporting material.
  11. Mr. Ortiz: I would like to check the record first. How long do I have to request that review, and where should I find the precise instructions?
  12. Leena: The review window is five school days from the decision notice. I will send the instructions with the record information you are authorized to receive.
  13. Mr. Ortiz: If I identify an incorrect fact, should I send a whole new application, or just explain the issue and attach the relevant evidence?
  14. Leena: Identify the disputed fact and attach the relevant supporting document. The review team needs a clear account of the issue. A new application is not what this procedure asks for.
  15. Mr. Ortiz: If I send that document today, what will your reply actually confirm? I do not want to mistake a receipt for a changed decision.
  16. Leena: An acknowledgment confirms receipt, not the outcome. The review team still needs to examine the identified fact. I cannot promise that the result will change.
  17. Mr. Ortiz: Thank you. I had also assumed the placement discussion would happen now, but perhaps that is a different stage from this application review.
  18. Leena: Yes. Placement concerns the class or setting, rather than whether a place has been offered. We should keep those stages separate while the admissions position remains unchanged.
  19. Mr. Ortiz: Please send the procedure, the permitted record information, and confirmation that she remains fourth. I will check for any factual error before deciding about review.
  20. Leena: I will include the relevant decision notice details. Your daughter met the entry criteria; the lottery did not select her; and no vacancy is currently confirmed. Those are the facts we can rely on.
Dialogue in the book, page 10 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Explain the applicant's process, not other applicants

Fictional admissions inquiry using the lesson case: a family asks why an applicant was not offered a place; the administrator has not reviewed the applicant-specific decision record.

  1. Family liaison: The family wants to know why their applicant wasn't offered a place. What can we explain?
  2. Admissions officer: First clarify whether they want the published admissions criteria, their decision record, or the review process.
  3. Family liaison: They asked whether another applicant took the place. Should we compare the two applications for them?
  4. Admissions officer: No. We should explain their own process without discussing another applicant's information.
  5. Family liaison: Can we say limited capacity caused the decision, even though we haven't reviewed this applicant's record?
  6. Admissions officer: Not yet. Capacity may be relevant generally, but we must not invent this decision's reason.
  7. Family liaison: I'll ask which part of the process they need clarified and route the applicant-specific question appropriately.
  8. Admissions officer: Good. Use the published process and the authorized record, keeping any unknown reason explicitly unresolved.
  9. Family liaison: That lets us address equity concerns without making unsupported comparisons between children.
  10. Admissions officer: Exactly. I'll identify the appropriate admissions contact so the family receives a clear, individual response.
Language placement is not an admissions decision

Fictional school inquiry: an admitted learner awaits a language-placement review, and a family liaison asks whether admission itself is uncertain.

  1. Family liaison: The learner's placement review is pending. Does that mean the school hasn't admitted them yet?
  2. Enrollment coordinator: In this case admission is confirmed. The pending review concerns the appropriate language-learning group.
  3. Family liaison: Then I should distinguish admission from placement rather than tell the family their place is uncertain.
  4. Enrollment coordinator: Correct. Placement concerns the learning arrangement, while the admissions decision is already recorded here.
  5. Family liaison: Can I promise the advanced group if the family says the learner studied English previously?
  6. Enrollment coordinator: No. That information can support the review, but it doesn't establish its outcome.
  7. Family liaison: What focused question should I ask the placement team before speaking with the family?
  8. Enrollment coordinator: Ask which information remains needed and how the family will receive the reviewed placement decision.
  9. Family liaison: I'll avoid guessing a group or deadline that the team hasn't confirmed.
  10. Enrollment coordinator: Thank you. A clear distinction can reassure the family without promising a placement we don't control.
A waiting-list position is not an offer date

Fictional enrollment discussion: a family has been told their applicant is on a waiting list; no future opening or offer date is confirmed.

  1. School receptionist: The family is on the waiting list. Can I tell them a place will open next month?
  2. Admissions administrator: No opening or offer date is confirmed. Could you clarify whether they are asking about status or likely timing?
  3. School receptionist: They want to plan transport, so an approximate date would help them make arrangements.
  4. Admissions administrator: We can acknowledge that need without inventing availability. Explain that waiting-list status isn't an offer.
  5. School receptionist: Should I ask how many other applicants are ahead and share their circumstances to explain the delay?
  6. Admissions administrator: Use only information authorized by the published process, and don't disclose other applicants' circumstances.
  7. School receptionist: I'll ask the admissions team what status information can be provided for this specific inquiry.
  8. Admissions administrator: Good. Also distinguish confirmed capacity from possible changes that haven't resulted in an available place.
  9. School receptionist: The family will hear that timing remains unknown and receive the correct contact for updates.
  10. Admissions administrator: That is clear and fair, without an unsupported date or comparison with another applicant.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

Eligible, but no place available yet
  1. Read the completed conversation in pairs; stress eligible, lottery, and fourth.
  2. Repeat turns 9-16, distinguishing the review request from its acknowledgment.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

A different list, the same distinction

A fictional course has 12 places and 15 eligible applicants. An applicant not selected by lottery is second on its waiting list. No vacancy is confirmed. Its local review covers factual errors only.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Officer: "The applicant met the ___."

Answer and explanation

C. entry criteria

The brief explicitly confirms eligibility, meaning the entry conditions were met.

2. Guardian: "The current waiting-list position is ___."

Answer and explanation

B. second

The supplied position is second, not a prediction of waiting time.

3. Officer: "The review concerns a claimed ___."

Answer and explanation

A. factual error

The fictional procedure limits review to a disputed fact or record.

4. Guardian: "A future offer is ___."

Answer and explanation

A. not confirmed

No vacancy is confirmed, so a future place cannot be promised.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Build vocabulary and collocations — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

Lesson 01: Enrollment, Admissions, and Placement
Original fictional case: Admissions officer Leena speaks with guardian Mr. Ortiz about a fictional program with 20 places and 24 eligible applicants. The published process uses a lottery among eligible applicants. His child met the entry criteria but was not selected and is fourth on the waiting list. The local procedure allows a factual-error review requested within five school days of the decision notice. A review is not a second lottery, and no vacancy is confirmed. Leena may explain the family's own record and published process, not other applicants' information.
Language workshop: Eligible, but no place available yet
Communication goal: Explain an allocation decision without disclosing other applicants or promising a future offer.
Language guidance: Eligibility means the applicant meets the conditions for consideration. It does not guarantee selection in an oversubscribed program. Selection, acceptance of an offer, enrollment, and class placement should not be treated as interchangeable.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): "I understand why this outcome is disappointing." / "Your child met the published entry criteria." / "Eligibility did not guarantee a place in this intake." / "There were 20 places for 24 eligible applicants." / "The published procedure used a lottery." / "Your child is fourth on the waiting list." / "No vacancy is confirmed at present." / "I cannot discuss another applicant's record." / "I can explain your child's record and the published process." / "The review examines a claimed factual error." / "A review is not a second lottery." / "The local window is five school days from the notice." / "Please identify the disputed fact and supporting document." / "We will acknowledge your review request." / "Enrollment begins only after the relevant offer steps." / "I will send the procedure and confirm the current waiting-list position."
Vocabulary: admissions: The process of considering applicants for entry. / applicant: A person seeking entry to a program. / eligibility: Meeting the conditions for consideration. / entry criteria: The published conditions an applicant must satisfy. / selection: Choosing among applicants for available places. / capacity: The number of places available. / oversubscribed: Having more applicants than available places. / allocation: Assignment of available places under a process. / lottery: A random selection process among an eligible group. / offer: A formal invitation to take an available place. / waiting list: A list of applicants awaiting possible vacancies. / vacancy: An available, unfilled place. / decision notice: The communication of an application outcome. / factual error: An incorrect statement or record of fact. / review request: A request to examine a decision under a stated process. / review window: The period allowed for requesting review. / school day: A day counted under the institution's school-day definition. / supporting document: Material supplied to substantiate a claim. / enrollment: Formal registration after the applicable admission steps. / placement: Assignment to an appropriate class, level, or setting. / intake: A group admitted during a particular entry period. / confidential record: Information subject to restricted access or disclosure. / published procedure: An officially available description of the process. / acknowledgment: Confirmation that a message or request was received.
Speaking task: Read the completed conversation in pairs; stress eligible, lottery, and fourth. Repeat turns 9-16, distinguishing the review request from its acknowledgment. Switch roles and read the four completed transfer lines, retaining the second waiting-list position.
Partner: Leena: Admissions officer / Mr. Ortiz: Guardian

Practice grammar in context — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Lesson 01: Enrollment, Admissions, and Placement
Original fictional case: Admissions officer Leena speaks with guardian Mr. Ortiz about a fictional program with 20 places and 24 eligible applicants. The published process uses a lottery among eligible applicants. His child met the entry criteria but was not selected and is fourth on the waiting list. The local procedure allows a factual-error review requested within five school days of the decision notice. A review is not a second lottery, and no vacancy is confirmed. Leena may explain the family's own record and published process, not other applicants' information.
Language workshop: Eligible, but no place available yet
Communication goal: Explain an allocation decision without disclosing other applicants or promising a future offer.
Language guidance: Eligibility means the applicant meets the conditions for consideration. It does not guarantee selection in an oversubscribed program. Selection, acceptance of an offer, enrollment, and class placement should not be treated as interchangeable.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): "I understand why this outcome is disappointing." / "Your child met the published entry criteria." / "Eligibility did not guarantee a place in this intake." / "There were 20 places for 24 eligible applicants." / "The published procedure used a lottery." / "Your child is fourth on the waiting list." / "No vacancy is confirmed at present." / "I cannot discuss another applicant's record." / "I can explain your child's record and the published process." / "The review examines a claimed factual error." / "A review is not a second lottery." / "The local window is five school days from the notice." / "Please identify the disputed fact and supporting document." / "We will acknowledge your review request." / "Enrollment begins only after the relevant offer steps." / "I will send the procedure and confirm the current waiting-list position."
Vocabulary: admissions: The process of considering applicants for entry. / applicant: A person seeking entry to a program. / eligibility: Meeting the conditions for consideration. / entry criteria: The published conditions an applicant must satisfy. / selection: Choosing among applicants for available places. / capacity: The number of places available. / oversubscribed: Having more applicants than available places. / allocation: Assignment of available places under a process. / lottery: A random selection process among an eligible group. / offer: A formal invitation to take an available place. / waiting list: A list of applicants awaiting possible vacancies. / vacancy: An available, unfilled place. / decision notice: The communication of an application outcome. / factual error: An incorrect statement or record of fact. / review request: A request to examine a decision under a stated process. / review window: The period allowed for requesting review. / school day: A day counted under the institution's school-day definition. / supporting document: Material supplied to substantiate a claim. / enrollment: Formal registration after the applicable admission steps. / placement: Assignment to an appropriate class, level, or setting. / intake: A group admitted during a particular entry period. / confidential record: Information subject to restricted access or disclosure. / published procedure: An officially available description of the process. / acknowledgment: Confirmation that a message or request was received.
Speaking task: Read the completed conversation in pairs; stress eligible, lottery, and fourth. Repeat turns 9-16, distinguishing the review request from its acknowledgment. Switch roles and read the four completed transfer lines, retaining the second waiting-list position.
Partner: Leena: Admissions officer / Mr. Ortiz: Guardian
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A fictional course has 12 places and 15 eligible applicants. An applicant not selected by lottery is second on its waiting list. No vacancy is confirmed. Its local review covers factual errors only.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Mr. Ortiz: Thank you. I had also assumed the placement discussion would happen now, but perhaps that is a different stage from this application review.
Leena: Yes. Placement concerns the class or setting, rather than whether a place has been offered. We should keep those stages separate while the admissions position remains unchanged.
Mr. Ortiz: Please send the procedure, the permitted record information, and confirmation that she remains fourth. I will check for any factual error before deciding about review.
Leena: I will include the relevant decision notice details. Your daughter met the entry criteria; the lottery did not select her; and no vacancy is currently confirmed. Those are the facts we can rely on.
END REFERENCE
Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

02Curriculum Planning and Learning OutcomesA topic list is not a learning outcome+

Your goal

Align a measurable learning outcome with classroom preparation, assessment evidence, and a shared scoring rule.

This lesson in the book, page 13 ↗

A Read the situation

A topic list is not a learning outcome

Curriculum coordinator Pavel and teacher Nia review a draft course before teaching begins. Its topic list includes media literacy, but the proposed quiz asks only for definitions. The agreed intended outcome is to compare two supplied sources and justify which is more reliable using authorship and supporting evidence. The draft lessons currently practice only recalling terms. The team can revise the lessons and assessment before approval. They need source-comparison practice and a task scored on both named criteria, not a claim that remembering definitions demonstrates evaluation.

Pavel: Curriculum coordinator / Nia: Teacher

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What must learners demonstrate in the intended outcome?

Answer and explanation

D. Compare two sources and justify reliability using authorship and evidence.

The agreed outcome explicitly requires comparison and justification using two named criteria.

2. What does the proposed quiz currently assess?

Answer and explanation

A. Recall of definitions

The supplied quiz asks for definitions rather than the intended comparative judgment.

3. When can the team make the proposed changes?

Answer and explanation

C. Before teaching begins and before draft approval

The brief places the course in a draft stage before instruction and approval.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

A topic names what a course covers; an outcome states what learners will do. "Media literacy" is a topic. Comparing two sources and justifying reliability against specified criteria is an observable outcome.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
curriculum
The planned learning content and experiences of a program.review the curriculum
topic
A subject area covered in teaching.list course topics
learning outcome
An observable performance learners should achieve.specify a learning outcome
observable verb
An action word that identifies demonstrable performance.choose an observable verb
alignment
A match among outcomes, teaching, and assessment.check curriculum alignment
backward design
Planning from intended outcomes to evidence and instruction.use backward design
assessment
A task or process used to gather evidence of learning.design an assessment
formative assessment
Evidence used during learning to guide improvement.use formative assessment
summative assessment
Evidence used to judge achievement at a designated point.plan summative assessment
assessment criterion
A stated feature used to judge performance.define assessment criteria
rubric
Descriptions of performance levels against criteria.apply a shared rubric
descriptor
A statement describing performance at a particular level.clarify a descriptor
authorship
Information about who produced a source.evaluate authorship
supporting evidence
Material that supports a source's claims.examine supporting evidence
source reliability
The extent to which a source merits trust for a purpose.assess source reliability
justification
Reasons supporting a judgment or choice.provide a justification
comparison
Examination of similarities and differences.make a source comparison
recall
Retrieval of previously learned information.assess factual recall
application
Use of knowledge in a task or context.demonstrate application
evaluation
A judgment made against stated criteria.demonstrate evaluation
practice task
An activity preparing learners for a target performance.sequence practice tasks
exemplar
A sample illustrating expected or particular-quality work.discuss an exemplar
scaffolding
Structured support that helps learners perform a task.provide appropriate scaffolding
curriculum map
A view linking program elements, outcomes, and assessment.update the curriculum map

Recall can support evaluation, but recalling definitions alone does not demonstrate a supported judgment. Revise both practice and assessment. In this case the course is still a draft, so the changes precede instruction and grading.

C Notice the language

Challenge alignment, not a colleague's competence

A familiar quiz may be well written but measure the wrong performance. State the intended learner action and show the mismatch. This makes revision a shared design problem, not a personal judgment about a teacher or an argument against all factual knowledge.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Name the gap
"The topic is clear, but the learner performance is not."
State the intended action
"Learners must compare two supplied sources."
Specify the judgment
"They must justify which source is more reliable."
Name the criteria
"Use authorship and supporting evidence."
Identify the current measure
"The quiz currently measures recall."
Preserve useful knowledge
"Definitions support the task but do not complete it."
Connect instruction
"Lessons need practice in the same kind of comparison."
Choose suitable evidence
"A supported comparison would demonstrate the intended outcome."
Clarify scoring
"The rubric should address both named criteria."
Define a descriptor
"State what adequate use of evidence looks like."
Use an example
"Review an exemplar before independent practice."
Offer bounded support
"Provide the sources and a comparison framework."
Avoid a proxy
"Response length is not the stated criterion."
Locate the revision stage
"We can revise this before teaching begins."
Record the link
"Update the curriculum map with the aligned task."
Confirm the decision
"The outcome, practice, and assessment now require the same performance."
6 language and precision notes
Know
Often too broad unless a demonstrable performance is specified.
Compare
Requires examining sources in relation to each other.
Justify
Requires reasons, not just a selected answer.
Criteria
The dimensions of judgment, not the topic headings.
Aligned
Instruction and assessment support the intended outcome.
Draft
Changes can be approved before learners are taught and assessed.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which assessment matches the intended outcome?

Answer and explanation

D. Compare the supplied sources using authorship and evidence, then justify the stronger source.

The correct task directly elicits comparison and justification against both required criteria.

2. Which instructional change is needed?

Answer and explanation

D. Add guided source comparisons before the final assessment.

Learners need preparation in the performance that the intended assessment will require.

3. Which proposed scoring feature is unsupported?

Answer and explanation

A. Awarding marks only for response length

Length alone is not one of the agreed criteria and does not establish sound evaluation.

4. Which statement preserves an appropriate role for definitions?

Answer and explanation

B. Definitions can support comparison but are insufficient evidence of the complete outcome.

Factual knowledge can enable higher-level work without demonstrating that the work occurred.

E Conversations

A topic list is not a learning outcome

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Pavel: Curriculum coordinator / Nia: Teacher

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • alignment
  • curriculum map
  • learning outcome
  • exemplar
  • assessment criterion
  • evaluation
  • descriptor
  • recall
  • rubric
  • practice task
  1. Pavel
    The draft curriculum lists media literacy, and the quiz is ready. Before we approve it, can we state what learners must actually do with the material?
  2. Nia
    The intended 1 is to compare two supplied sources and justify which is more reliable using authorship and supporting evidence. That is more specific than covering the topic.
  3. Pavel
    At the moment the quiz asks for definitions of reliability, evidence, and author. Those questions are clear, but do they show the performance we agreed?
  4. Nia
    They show 2, not the complete comparison. A learner could reproduce every definition without applying either criterion to the sources. We need a different final task.
  5. Pavel
    I would keep the definitions as a starter quiz. Could we use them to check the vocabulary before students tackle the sources?
  6. Nia
    Certainly. The issue is 3, not whether definitions matter. The outcome, classroom practice, and assessment should reinforce the same performance, with vocabulary serving that performance rather than replacing it.
  7. Pavel
    Our lessons practice definitions too. Changing only the final assessment would confront learners with a comparison task they had not practiced.
  8. Nia
    Add a guided 4 with two sources. We can model how authorship and supporting evidence affect the judgment, then gradually reduce the support before the assessed task.
  9. Pavel
    For the final task, I suggest supplying both sources and asking for a choice with reasons under the two named headings. That keeps the evidence focused.
  10. Nia
    Yes. Each 5 should be visible in the task and the scoring guidance. We should not reward a long response that never evaluates either source.
  11. Pavel
    Some teachers suggest one overall mark for sounding persuasive. That seems hard to explain consistently without mentioning the two required criteria.
  12. Nia
    A shared 6 should describe how well the learner uses authorship information and supporting evidence. Persuasive wording alone cannot substitute for an accurate, supported comparison.
  13. Pavel
    The draft says sufficient evidence. One teacher expects a quotation; another expects an explanation. We need to settle that before anyone marks it.
  14. Nia
    Write a clear 7 for each level. It should distinguish naming a feature from explaining why that feature strengthens or weakens a source for this purpose.
  15. Pavel
    Could we use a sample response during teaching? Students might understand the distinction more quickly if they could see how a reason connects to a judgment.
  16. Nia
    An 8 would help. We can identify the evidence supporting its conclusion, rather than asking students to copy its wording or assume there is only one acceptable sentence.
  17. Pavel
    This is still a draft, so we can revise before teaching starts. I will check that the lesson sequence, task instructions, and scoring guidance all match.
  18. Nia
    Update the 9 as well. That will show where learners practice the comparison and where they demonstrate it, instead of leaving the revised quiz disconnected from the plan.
  19. Pavel
    Our approval summary will say that learners compare two supplied sources, apply both criteria, and justify the stronger source. Definitions remain supporting knowledge, not the final evidence.
  20. Nia
    That captures the required 10. We will approve the revised design only after the practice and scoring materials are ready, so students receive a coherent learning experience from the start.

Answers and explanations
  1. learning outcome Turn 2. The learning outcome specifies the observable source-comparison performance, rather than merely naming the media-literacy topic.
  2. recall Turn 4. Recall demonstrates retrieval of definitions, which is useful but does not itself demonstrate comparative evaluation.
  3. alignment Turn 6. Alignment requires the outcome, instructional practice, and assessment to support the same intended learner performance.
  4. practice task Turn 8. A practice task prepares learners for the comparison and justification they will later need to demonstrate independently.
  5. assessment criterion Turn 10. An assessment criterion identifies a dimension of performance to judge, here authorship or supporting evidence.
  6. rubric Turn 12. The rubric describes performance against the required criteria, providing a clearer basis for consistent scoring than an undefined impression.
  7. descriptor Turn 14. A descriptor explains a performance level so teachers do not rely on different unstated interpretations of quality.
  8. exemplar Turn 16. An exemplar makes the expected performance visible and supports discussion of how criteria are applied.
  9. curriculum map Turn 18. The curriculum map records the links between outcomes, teaching experiences, and assessment so the revision remains traceable.
  10. evaluation Turn 20. Evaluation is the supported judgment against criteria; it is more than recalling terms or selecting a source without reasons.
Read the complete conversation

Dialogue in the book, page 19 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

A topic is not an observable learning outcome

Fictional curriculum meeting using the lesson case: a plan lists topics, but teachers disagree about what the assessment should measure.

  1. Curriculum coordinator: The plan lists persuasive writing. What should learners be able to do that we can actually observe?
  2. Teacher: They should state a clear claim and support it with reasons in a short written argument.
  3. Curriculum coordinator: That is a proposed learning outcome, while persuasive writing is the broader topic label.
  4. Teacher: Then a spelling quiz alone wouldn't show whether learners can support a written claim.
  5. Curriculum coordinator: Correct. The assessment should provide evidence of the outcome we agree, not merely relate to the same topic.
  6. Teacher: Should we place that outcome in the curriculum map and check the relevant program standard?
  7. Curriculum coordinator: Yes. We need to confirm alignment rather than claim the proposal already satisfies an unreviewed standard.
  8. Teacher: I'll bring a sample writing task so colleagues can discuss what evidence it would produce.
  9. Curriculum coordinator: Good. Let's agree the outcome before debating marks for a task that may measure something else.
  10. Teacher: I'll label it a proposal and invite the other teachers to refine the observable performance.
Completing an activity does not prove the outcome

Fictional curriculum review: learners completed a group poster, while the intended outcome is explaining a cause-and-effect relationship individually.

  1. Department head: Every group finished a poster. Can we mark the cause-and-effect outcome as achieved for every learner?
  2. Assessment coordinator: Poster completion is evidence of finishing the activity, not necessarily each learner's ability to explain the relationship.
  3. Department head: So the product and the individual learning outcome aren't the same thing in our curriculum map.
  4. Assessment coordinator: Exactly. The group artifact may contribute evidence, but individual understanding still needs an appropriate check.
  5. Department head: Would a short individual explanation align more directly with the stated outcome than counting finished posters?
  6. Assessment coordinator: That is a useful proposal. Teachers should review its accessibility and criteria before using it.
  7. Department head: I won't describe all learners as having mastered the outcome based solely on group completion.
  8. Assessment coordinator: Good. We can recognize the completed work while keeping the learning claim appropriately bounded.
  9. Department head: I'll bring the proposed explanation task to the team and ask what performance would meet the criterion.
  10. Assessment coordinator: Then the assessment discussion will focus on observable learning rather than completion alone.
Coverage in a curriculum map is not depth of learning

Fictional program review: a topic appears in three units of a curriculum map, but the intended progression between units is unspecified.

  1. Program director: The topic appears in three units. Does that show a clear progression in our curriculum?
  2. Curriculum specialist: It shows repeated coverage, but we haven't specified how the learning outcome develops across those units.
  3. Program director: Could you explain progression in plain English so teachers know what we need to examine?
  4. Curriculum specialist: It means identifying what learners should do with greater complexity or independence at each stage.
  5. Program director: Then repeating the same topic title doesn't establish that later assessments demand deeper understanding.
  6. Curriculum specialist: Correct. We should compare the intended performances and their assessment evidence, not just count map entries.
  7. Program director: I'll ask each unit owner to explain the observable outcome and what prior learning it assumes.
  8. Curriculum specialist: Please also flag assumptions that aren't supported by earlier assessment information.
  9. Program director: That will let us identify possible gaps without claiming learners have failed anything yet.
  10. Curriculum specialist: Exactly. We'll revise the progression proposal after reviewing the actual outcomes and evidence requirements together.

Match the task to the outcome

A draft course outcome requires learners to classify four supplied expenses as fixed or variable and explain each classification. The current quiz only asks for the two definitions. Teaching has not begun.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Coordinator: "The quiz currently tests ___."

Answer and explanation

C. recall

Asking for definitions measures retrieval rather than the required classification with reasons.

2. Teacher: "The revised task must include ___."

Answer and explanation

B. classification

The stated outcome requires sorting each supplied expense into a category.

3. Coordinator: "Each choice also needs an ___."

Answer and explanation

A. explanation

The outcome requires reasons for classifications, not only category labels.

4. Teacher: "We should revise practice and assessment before ___."

Answer and explanation

A. teaching begins

The course is still a draft, allowing alignment before students encounter instruction.

Build vocabulary and collocations — complete prompt

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Lesson 02: Curriculum Planning and Learning Outcomes
Original fictional case: Curriculum coordinator Pavel and teacher Nia review a draft course before teaching begins. Its topic list includes media literacy, but the proposed quiz asks only for definitions. The agreed intended outcome is to compare two supplied sources and justify which is more reliable using authorship and supporting evidence. The draft lessons currently practice only recalling terms. The team can revise the lessons and assessment before approval. They need source-comparison practice and a task scored on both named criteria, not a claim that remembering definitions demonstrates evaluation.
Language workshop: A topic list is not a learning outcome
Communication goal: Align a measurable learning outcome with classroom preparation, assessment evidence, and a shared scoring rule.
Language guidance: A topic names what a course covers; an outcome states what learners will do. "Media literacy" is a topic. Comparing two sources and justifying reliability against specified criteria is an observable outcome.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): "The topic is clear, but the learner performance is not." / "Learners must compare two supplied sources." / "They must justify which source is more reliable." / "Use authorship and supporting evidence." / "The quiz currently measures recall." / "Definitions support the task but do not complete it." / "Lessons need practice in the same kind of comparison." / "A supported comparison would demonstrate the intended outcome." / "The rubric should address both named criteria." / "State what adequate use of evidence looks like." / "Review an exemplar before independent practice." / "Provide the sources and a comparison framework." / "Response length is not the stated criterion." / "We can revise this before teaching begins." / "Update the curriculum map with the aligned task." / "The outcome, practice, and assessment now require the same performance."
Vocabulary: curriculum: The planned learning content and experiences of a program. / topic: A subject area covered in teaching. / learning outcome: An observable performance learners should achieve. / observable verb: An action word that identifies demonstrable performance. / alignment: A match among outcomes, teaching, and assessment. / backward design: Planning from intended outcomes to evidence and instruction. / assessment: A task or process used to gather evidence of learning. / formative assessment: Evidence used during learning to guide improvement. / summative assessment: Evidence used to judge achievement at a designated point. / assessment criterion: A stated feature used to judge performance. / rubric: Descriptions of performance levels against criteria. / descriptor: A statement describing performance at a particular level. / authorship: Information about who produced a source. / supporting evidence: Material that supports a source's claims. / source reliability: The extent to which a source merits trust for a purpose. / justification: Reasons supporting a judgment or choice. / comparison: Examination of similarities and differences. / recall: Retrieval of previously learned information. / application: Use of knowledge in a task or context. / evaluation: A judgment made against stated criteria. / practice task: An activity preparing learners for a target performance. / exemplar: A sample illustrating expected or particular-quality work. / scaffolding: Structured support that helps learners perform a task. / curriculum map: A view linking program elements, outcomes, and assessment.
Speaking task: Read turns 1-8 with a pause after recall to make the assessment mismatch clear. Read turns 9-16, stressing authorship, supporting evidence, and descriptor. Switch roles for the transfer; pronounce classification and explanation as two separate requirements.
Partner: Pavel: Curriculum coordinator / Nia: Teacher
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A draft course outcome requires learners to classify four supplied expenses as fixed or variable and explain each classification. The current quiz only asks for the two definitions. Teaching has not begun.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Pavel: This is still a draft, so we can revise before teaching starts. I will check that the lesson sequence, task instructions, and scoring guidance all match.
Nia: Update the curriculum map as well. That will show where learners practice the comparison and where they demonstrate it, instead of leaving the revised quiz disconnected from the plan.
Pavel: Our approval summary will say that learners compare two supplied sources, apply both criteria, and justify the stronger source. Definitions remain supporting knowledge, not the final evidence.
Nia: That captures the required evaluation. We will approve the revised design only after the practice and scoring materials are ready, so students receive a coherent learning experience from the start.
END REFERENCE
Practice grammar in context — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.
