Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Two rubric interpretations need calibration

Fictional faculty coordination using the lesson case: two teachers interpret the same rubric differently and give inconsistent explanations to students.

  1. Department head: Students received different explanations of the same rubric level. Which interpretation did you each apply?
  2. Assessment lead: One teacher required two supporting examples, while the other accepted one well-developed example under the same wording.
  3. Department head: That identifies the observed difference more clearly than saying the teachers grade unfairly.
  4. Assessment lead: Yes. The effect is inconsistent guidance, but we haven't yet reviewed every affected mark.
  5. Department head: Could we compare anonymized examples together and agree how the rubric wording should be interpreted?
  6. Assessment lead: That would support calibration, meaning a shared understanding of how to apply the criteria.
  7. Department head: Please check the grading policy before proposing any changes to marks or communicating a correction.
  8. Assessment lead: Agreed. The review should distinguish interpretation, any verified marking effect, and the authorized response.
  9. Department head: I'll arrange a focused professional-development discussion with both teachers rather than attribute motives to either.
  10. Assessment lead: I'll bring the rubric and examples so the next action addresses the specific observed inconsistency.
An observation note needs a concrete behavior

Fictional faculty feedback: an observation note says a teacher did not engage learners; the observer recorded no learner counts but noted one unanswered invitation to speak.

  1. Faculty mentor: Your observation note says learners were disengaged. What specific behavior supports that description?
  2. Observer: I noted one invitation to speak followed by silence, but I didn't record broader participation counts.
  3. Faculty mentor: Then the note should not turn that single moment into a conclusion about every learner.
  4. Observer: I can describe the invitation and silence without claiming I know why nobody responded.
  5. Faculty mentor: Exactly. Explain the effect you observed and invite the teacher's perspective before proposing improvement.
  6. Observer: Would asking about wait time and alternative ways to participate be a constructive next step?
  7. Faculty mentor: Yes, as questions for professional development, not proof that a specific teaching approach failed.
  8. Observer: I'll revise the observation language and discuss that moment with the teacher privately.
  9. Faculty mentor: Keep any formal evaluation conclusion within the applicable process and supported evidence.
  10. Observer: Understood. My feedback will name the observed event, acknowledge its limits, and invite a practical discussion.
Different feedback at the same rubric level

Fictional marking review: two teachers awarded the same rubric level, but one wrote needs more evidence and the other wrote explain your examples.

  1. Course coordinator: Both teachers awarded the same level, but students heard different advice about how to improve.
  2. Assessment specialist: One comment requests more evidence; the other asks learners to explain existing examples. Those imply different actions.
  3. Course coordinator: Does that establish different grading standards, or only a feedback-language inconsistency in these examples?
  4. Assessment specialist: Neither is established yet; different work may need different advice at the same level.
  5. Course coordinator: Then we should check whether each comment matches the needs shown in the corresponding work.
  6. Assessment specialist: Exactly. Let's compare each comment with the work and rubric criterion before deciding whether clarification is needed.
  7. Course coordinator: Could we model specific feedback during the next professional-development meeting using anonymized work?
  8. Assessment specialist: Yes. That makes the next action practical while protecting student information and avoiding personal blame.
  9. Course coordinator: I'll ask both teachers to explain their intended meaning before we propose any shared guidance.
  10. Assessment specialist: I'll bring the criterion and examples so the discussion stays grounded in the observed feedback.

A criterion is not a total

A rubric has two criteria worth five points each. Review confirms four points for the first criterion. The second has not been reviewed. An unstated length penalty may also have affected six other papers.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

Answer and explanation

Answer and explanation

D. four

The supplied review establishes four points only for the first criterion.

3. Lead: "The final corrected total remains ___."

Answer and explanation

D. unresolved

The second criterion is not yet reviewed, so the total cannot be inferred.

4. Teacher: "The six potentially affected papers need ___."

Answer and explanation

A. review

Possible use of the same unsupported penalty requires examination, not automatic identical changes.

Sam: Our message can say that we are reviewing inconsistent use of the rubric. It should not say that a new scoring rule has been introduced.
Erin: Correct. This is calibration of the published standard. We will document the interpretation, review affected work, and communicate any verified corrections through the agreed process.
END REFERENCE

Lesson 05: Faculty Coordination and Evaluation
Original fictional case: Department lead Erin and teacher Sam compare scores for the same anonymized student paper. The published rubric has three criteria, each scored from zero to four, for a maximum of twelve. Erin awarded ten and Sam seven. For the evidence criterion, the level-three descriptor requires two relevant examples with an explanation of how each supports the claim. The paper supplies both, but Sam reduced that criterion because the response was short. Length is not a criterion. They must calibrate the rubric, review affected work, and explain any corrected scores under the institution's process.
Language workshop: The same paper, two different rubric readings
Communication goal: Resolve scoring disagreements through shared criteria and evidence rather than averaging incompatible judgments.
Language guidance: A total of ten versus seven signals disagreement but does not reveal which criterion is wrong. Examine the criterion-level judgments. Averaging the totals to 8.5 would conceal the identified unsupported length penalty rather than resolve it.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): "We scored the same paper differently." / "There are three criteria, each worth up to four." / "Level three requires two relevant examples with explanation." / "Both examples are connected to the claim." / "Length is not part of this rubric." / "A short answer can still meet the descriptor." / "Averaging totals would not resolve the interpretation." / "Let us calibrate against this anonymized paper." / "The score needs a criterion-based explanation." / "We have resolved this criterion, not the entire total." / "Check other work affected by the same interpretation." / "Apply the published criteria rather than adding a new rule." / "Document any adjustment and its reason." / "The comment should explain the criterion actually used." / "We are reviewing inconsistent application of the rubric." / "Confirm corrected scores through the institution's process."
Vocabulary: grading rubric: A scoring framework with criteria and performance descriptions. / criterion: A dimension on which work is judged. / performance level: A defined degree of achievement. / score range: The permitted interval of marks. / maximum score: The highest available mark. / evidence criterion: A scoring dimension concerning support for a claim. / relevant example: An example connected to the point being made. / claim: A statement or position supported by reasons. / rationale: The reason for a judgment or decision. / calibration: Developing a shared application of a scoring standard. / moderation: Reviewing assessment judgments for consistency and fairness. / anchor paper: A reference sample used to illustrate a scoring level. / anonymized work: Work presented without identifiers that reveal the learner. / inter-rater agreement: The extent to which different scorers agree. / scoring discrepancy: A difference between judgments of the same work. / unsupported penalty: A deduction not justified by the applicable criteria. / reassessment: A fresh evaluation of work under the relevant process. / affected cohort: The group whose work may be influenced by an issue. / feedback comment: An explanation given to a learner about work. / consistency check: A review of whether a standard is applied uniformly. / score adjustment: A change to a recorded mark. / audit trail: A record showing changes and their reasons. / professional judgment: Informed evaluation using relevant expertise and standards. / standardization: Establishing shared procedures or interpretations.
Speaking task: Read turns 1-8; pronounce criterion and criteria accurately and stress the two required examples. Repeat turns 11-16, keeping the confirmed criterion score separate from the unconfirmed total. Switch roles for the transfer and read ten and four distinctly.
Partner: Erin: Department lead / Sam: Teacher
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A rubric has two criteria worth five points each. Review confirms four points for the first criterion. The second has not been reviewed. An unstated length penalty may also have affected six other papers.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Sam: We will need a record of the original score, the reviewed score, and the reason. Otherwise later questions could make the correction difficult to explain.

Erin: Correct. This is calibration of the published standard. We will document the interpretation, review affected work, and communicate any verified corrections through the agreed process.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

06Compliance, Records, and PrivacyA route-planning task does not need an entire file+

Your goal

Clarify purpose, authorized access, and minimum task-relevant records without treating job title as blanket permission.

This lesson in the book, page 49 ↗

A Read the situation

A route-planning task does not need an entire file

Transport coordinator Dana asks records officer Yusuf for complete student files to update pickup assignments. The task requires student IDs and assigned pickup stops only; counseling notes, disciplinary history, and health information are not needed for this task. The fictional institution requires the records owner to approve the fields, recipients, and access period before release. That approval is pending. Yusuf can prepare a two-field roster for review but cannot release it yet. An old shared folder still grants broad access and must be referred for access review, not assumed safe because it is internal.

Dana: Transport coordinator / Yusuf: Records officer

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. Which fields are needed for the stated task?

Answer and explanation

B. Student IDs and assigned pickup stops

The brief limits route-assignment needs to two specified fields.

2. What is the current release status?

Answer and explanation

B. Approval is pending, so no roster may be released yet.

The fictional local rule requires approval before release, and that approval is pending.

3. What should happen to the old broad-access folder?

Answer and explanation

A. Refer its access for review.

Broad existing access needs review rather than being treated as proof of appropriate authorization.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Reducing a file to two fields addresses relevance but does not itself authorize release. In this case the records owner must approve fields, recipients, and access period. The approval remains pending even for the limited roster.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
education record
Recorded information about a learner maintained in an educational context.handle education records
record custodian
The role responsible for maintaining specified records.contact the record custodian
records owner
The accountable authority for a dataset or record collection.obtain records-owner approval
authorized recipient
A person approved to receive specified information.verify authorized recipients
legitimate educational interest
A U.S. FERPA access concept tied to relevant professional responsibilities.assess legitimate educational interest
FERPA
The U.S. Family Educational Rights and Privacy Act.consult applicable FERPA guidance
purpose limitation
Restricting information use to an authorized purpose.state the access purpose
data minimization
Limiting data to what is necessary for the purpose.apply data minimization
field
A defined item of information in a record.approve the requested fields
roster
A list of people or identifiers with relevant details.prepare a limited roster
student identifier
A value used to distinguish a student record.verify the student identifier
pickup assignment
The stop or arrangement assigned for a learner's transport.update pickup assignments
sensitive information
Information requiring particular care because of potential harm or rules.restrict sensitive information
counseling note
A record of a counseling interaction or observation.protect counseling notes
disciplinary record
Documentation of disciplinary matters.restrict disciplinary records
access scope
The information and actions an authorization permits.define the access scope
role-based access
Access assigned according to approved responsibilities.review role-based access
least privilege
Granting only the access needed for the authorized task.apply least privilege
access period
The time during which access is authorized.specify the access period
secure channel
An approved means of transmitting protected information.use a secure channel
redisclosure
Further sharing by a recipient.control redisclosure
retention schedule
Rules governing how long records are kept.follow the retention schedule
access review
A check of whether permissions remain appropriate.request an access review
release authorization
Approval to disclose specified information.confirm release authorization

FERPA is a U.S. law with a defined scope and exceptions, not a global label for all school privacy rules. Follow the applicable law and institutional procedure. Internal access and existing permissions do not by themselves establish that every use is appropriate.

C Notice the language

Explain the useful alternative

A privacy boundary is easier to understand when paired with a route to completing the work. State the task-relevant fields and the approval still required. Avoid implying that a colleague is untrustworthy merely because the request was broader than necessary.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Clarify the job
"Which information is needed to update pickup assignments?"
Narrow the fields
"The task needs student IDs and assigned pickup stops."
Explain the exclusion
"Counseling and disciplinary records are not needed for this task."
Separate relevance from permission
"A smaller roster still requires release approval."
Name the approval owner
"The records owner must approve the scope."
Identify recipients
"Please confirm who will receive the roster."
Set the time boundary
"The access period also needs approval."
State the current status
"Approval is pending, so I cannot release it yet."
Offer a practical step
"I can prepare the two-field roster for review."
Avoid title-based assumptions
"A job title is not blanket permission for the whole file."
Question inherited access
"Existing folder access needs review."
Keep the channel explicit
"Use the approved secure channel after authorization."
Limit further sharing
"Do not redistribute beyond the approved recipients."
Separate retention
"Access expiry does not replace the retention schedule."
Escalate appropriately
"I will refer the broad folder permissions for access review."
Confirm before release
"We need the approved fields, recipients, and period recorded."
6 language and precision notes
Internal
Describes location or audience, not automatic authorization.
Needed
Establishes task relevance, not permission on its own.
Prepared
Does not mean released.
Pending
The required decision has not yet been made.
Identifier
Can still be personal information; it is not automatically anonymous.
Expires
Refers to access here, not automatic record destruction.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which response best supports the transport task?

Answer and explanation

B. Prepare the ID-and-stop roster for owner review without releasing it.

The limited roster addresses the task while respecting the pending release authorization.

2. Why is the old folder not sufficient authority?

Answer and explanation

A. Existing access may be broader than the approved task requires.

Current permissions must be reviewed against purpose and authorization rather than assumed appropriate.

3. What must be approved under this fictional procedure?

Answer and explanation

B. Fields, recipients, and access period

The brief requires approval of all three named elements; a secure channel or a partially defined scope does not replace it.

4. Which statement about access expiry is accurate?

Answer and explanation

D. It does not by itself determine record retention or deletion.

The period of permitted access and the record-retention schedule address different decisions.

Answers and explanations
  1. access scope Turn 2. Access scope identifies the information and actions actually needed, instead of treating an existing folder as permission for every record.
  2. roster Turn 4. The roster is the limited list of student IDs and pickup stops that supports the stated task.
  3. records owner Turn 6. The records owner is the designated authority for approving the fields, recipients, and access period under the fictional procedure.
  4. authorized recipient Turn 8. An authorized recipient is approved for the specified information and purpose, rather than included through an indefinite team-wide request.
  5. purpose limitation Turn 10. Purpose limitation keeps use tied to the authorized pickup task instead of allowing unrelated uses of the same information.
  6. student identifier Turn 12. A student identifier can link the roster to a particular learner and is not automatically anonymous merely because it is not a name.
  7. retention schedule Turn 14. The retention schedule governs how long records are kept, a different question from how long a recipient may access them.
  8. access review Turn 16. An access review checks whether existing permissions remain appropriate; their existence alone is not proof of authorization for this use.
  9. redisclosure Turn 18. Redisclosure is further sharing by a recipient and must remain within the applicable approved conditions.
  10. release authorization Turn 20. Release authorization is the required approval still needed before the prepared roster can be disclosed.

Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

A narrow task does not establish full-file access

Fictional records request using the lesson case: a colleague asks for an entire student file for a narrow task, but authorized scope is unclear.

  1. Program administrator: Could you send the complete student record? I need to confirm one enrollment detail.
  2. Records officer: Please specify the detail and purpose so we can check the authorized scope and secure handling.
  3. Program administrator: Would being a school employee mean I can receive the whole file on a need-to-know basis?
  4. Records officer: Employment alone doesn't answer this request's scope. We should use the institution's access-review process.
  5. Program administrator: I only need the recorded enrollment date. I'll revise the request to that field.
  6. Records officer: Thank you. I'll verify authorization and the appropriate response channel rather than release unrelated information.
  7. Program administrator: Should I quote the Family Educational Rights and Privacy Act, or FERPA, as automatic permission?
  8. Records officer: No. Refer applicability and interpretation to the authorized specialist; this conversation isn't a legal determination.
  9. Program administrator: I'll state the task and requested timing, with access pending the proper review.
  10. Records officer: I'll confirm what can be shared for that purpose and identify any remaining authorization question.
A spreadsheet export includes unnecessary fields

Fictional school administration: a room-allocation task needs class counts, but a proposed export includes student names and personal contact details.

  1. Timetabling officer: The export includes names and contact details. Do we need those for allocating classroom sizes?
  2. Records coordinator: The stated task needs class counts. Please confirm whether an aggregate report would meet the requirement.
  3. Timetabling officer: Yes, counts by class should answer that question. I don't need individual contact information.
  4. Records coordinator: Then I'll request the aggregate view through our records process instead of circulating the full export.
  5. Timetabling officer: Could I keep the full file anyway in case another administrative question comes up later?
  6. Records coordinator: That future purpose is unspecified. We should assess actual need to know and authorized handling, not hypothetical convenience.
  7. Timetabling officer: Understood. Please provide the approved counts before the room-planning discussion if the review allows it.
  8. Records coordinator: I'll request that timing and flag any delay without promising access before authorization is checked.
  9. Timetabling officer: I'll keep the planning request limited to class counts and avoid copying the personal fields elsewhere.
  10. Records coordinator: Good. The response can support the task while keeping unrelated student-record information outside its scope.
The requested recipient is not yet verified

Fictional records inquiry: an email asks a school to send a learner's attendance record to a new address; recipient authority is unverified.

  1. School secretary: An email asks for an attendance record at a new address. Can I send it immediately?
  2. Records officer: First use the established identity and authorization checks. The new recipient's authority hasn't been verified.
  3. School secretary: The message knows the learner's name. Does that establish permission to receive the student record?
  4. Records officer: No. Knowing a name doesn't establish access rights or the appropriate secure-handling route.
  5. School secretary: What should my reply request without asking the sender to email more sensitive information unnecessarily?
  6. Records officer: Direct them to the school's approved verification process and identify the records contact who handles it.
  7. School secretary: Should I promise delivery today if they reply quickly, even before the review is complete?
  8. Records officer: No. Acknowledge the request and its review status without guaranteeing an unconfirmed release time.
  9. School secretary: I'll keep the record restricted and explain that verification must occur before sharing.
  10. Records officer: Thank you. We'll assess the recipient and scope through the proper process rather than infer authorization from familiarity.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

A route-planning task does not need an entire file
  1. Read turns 1-8, making the two permitted fields and named recipients easy to hear.
  2. Repeat turns 11-16, distinguishing removal of names, access expiry, and record retention.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Preparation is not permission

An examinations team needs student IDs and assigned rooms. Medical notes are not needed. Local policy requires the records owner to approve fields and recipients before release; approval is still pending.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Officer: "The relevant fields are IDs and ___."

Answer and explanation

B. assigned rooms

The stated examination task needs the room assignment, not unrelated records.

2. Coordinator: "The medical notes should be ___."

Answer and explanation

B. excluded

Medical notes are not needed for the narrowly defined task in this scenario.

3. Officer: "The required approval remains ___."

Answer and explanation

A. pending

The brief states that the records owner has not yet approved release.

4. Coordinator: "A prepared list must not yet be ___."

Answer and explanation

A. released

Preparation does not replace the required authorization before disclosure.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Lesson 06: Compliance, Records, and Privacy
Original fictional case: Transport coordinator Dana asks records officer Yusuf for complete student files to update pickup assignments. The task requires student IDs and assigned pickup stops only; counseling notes, disciplinary history, and health information are not needed for this task. The fictional institution requires the records owner to approve the fields, recipients, and access period before release. That approval is pending. Yusuf can prepare a two-field roster for review but cannot release it yet. An old shared folder still grants broad access and must be referred for access review, not assumed safe because it is internal.
Language workshop: A route-planning task does not need an entire file
Communication goal: Clarify purpose, authorized access, and minimum task-relevant records without treating job title as blanket permission.
Language guidance: Reducing a file to two fields addresses relevance but does not itself authorize release. In this case the records owner must approve fields, recipients, and access period. The approval remains pending even for the limited roster.

Vocabulary: education record: Recorded information about a learner maintained in an educational context. / record custodian: The role responsible for maintaining specified records. / records owner: The accountable authority for a dataset or record collection. / authorized recipient: A person approved to receive specified information. / legitimate educational interest: A U.S. FERPA access concept tied to relevant professional responsibilities. / FERPA: The U.S. Family Educational Rights and Privacy Act. / purpose limitation: Restricting information use to an authorized purpose. / data minimization: Limiting data to what is necessary for the purpose. / field: A defined item of information in a record. / roster: A list of people or identifiers with relevant details. / student identifier: A value used to distinguish a student record. / pickup assignment: The stop or arrangement assigned for a learner's transport. / sensitive information: Information requiring particular care because of potential harm or rules. / counseling note: A record of a counseling interaction or observation. / disciplinary record: Documentation of disciplinary matters. / access scope: The information and actions an authorization permits. / role-based access: Access assigned according to approved responsibilities. / least privilege: Granting only the access needed for the authorized task. / access period: The time during which access is authorized. / secure channel: An approved means of transmitting protected information. / redisclosure: Further sharing by a recipient. / retention schedule: Rules governing how long records are kept. / access review: A check of whether permissions remain appropriate. / release authorization: Approval to disclose specified information.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

07Accreditation and Program ReviewThree completed actions are not a closed review+

Your goal

Report action status, ownership, and evidence without confusing completion with effectiveness or accreditation approval.

This lesson in the book, page 58 ↗

A Read the situation

Three completed actions are not a closed review

Quality coordinator Amara and program director Hugh prepare a fictional internal review due on October 15. Its action plan has five actions. Actions 1, 2, and 3 have approved completion evidence. Action 4 is underway, with an assigned owner and an October 10 deadline. Action 5 has no assigned owner and no completion evidence. No outcome evaluation has yet established whether the completed actions improved learning. The team must report three of five actions complete, seek an owner and deadline for Action 5, and avoid presenting the internal review as an accreditation decision.

Amara: Quality coordinator / Hugh: Program director

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What share of actions is complete?

Answer and explanation

A. Three of five, or 60%

Three actions have approved completion evidence out of five total actions.

2. Which action lacks an owner?

Answer and explanation

B. Action 5

The brief identifies Action 5 as unassigned while Action 4 has an owner.

3. What remains unestablished about the completed actions?

Answer and explanation

D. Whether they improved learning outcomes

Completion is documented, but the brief states that outcome effectiveness has not been established.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Three completed actions out of five is 60%. Action 4 is underway, not complete, and Action 5 is unassigned. Counting an underway item as completed would inflate the rate to 80% without the required evidence.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
program review
A structured examination of a program's quality and performance.conduct a program review
accreditation
External recognition under a specified quality-assurance process.distinguish accreditation from internal review
self-study
An institution's documented examination of its own performance.prepare a self-study
review panel
A group examining a program against relevant expectations.brief the review panel
criterion for review
A standard used to evaluate a program.address a review criterion
supporting evidence
Material used to substantiate a review claim.organize supporting evidence
evidence register
An index connecting evidence to claims or requirements.maintain an evidence register
action plan
A set of improvement actions with responsibilities and timing.update the action plan
action owner
The person accountable for advancing an assigned action.assign an action owner
due date
The date by which an item is expected.confirm the due date
completion evidence
Information showing that an action was carried out.approve completion evidence
status report
A summary of current progress and unresolved items.issue a status report
underway
Started but not complete.report an action as underway
unassigned
Without a designated responsible person.flag an unassigned action
closure criterion
A condition required before an item is closed.verify closure criteria
effectiveness
The extent to which an action achieves its intended result.evaluate effectiveness
outcome measure
An indicator of the result being sought.select an outcome measure
implementation evidence
Information showing that a practice was put into use.examine implementation evidence
continuous improvement
Repeated use of evidence to improve practice.support continuous improvement
follow-up review
A later check of progress or results.schedule a follow-up review
evidence gap
Missing support for a claim or required conclusion.identify an evidence gap
accountability
Clear responsibility for actions and explanations.strengthen accountability
review finding
A conclusion reached through examination of evidence.distinguish a finding from a proposal
decision authority
The body or role permitted to make a decision.identify the decision authority

Completion evidence shows that an action occurred. Effectiveness evidence concerns whether the intended result followed. An internal action-plan update is neither proof of improved learning nor an external accreditation decision.

C Notice the language

A candid status report supports credible review

A review team needs a traceable account, not uniformly positive labels. Name what is complete, what is underway, and what has no owner. Acknowledging a remaining gap is more useful than describing an approved plan as if every action and outcome were already verified.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
State the verified count
"Three of five actions have approved completion evidence."
Translate the proportion
"That is 60% complete."
Separate underway work
"Action 4 is in progress, not closed."
Name the existing deadline
"Its assigned owner is working toward October 10."
Expose the ownership gap
"Action 5 remains unassigned."
Request a decision
"We need an owner and deadline for Action 5."
Keep evidence traceable
"Link each claim to its supporting record."
Avoid status inflation
"Do not count underway work as complete."
Separate action from result
"Completion does not yet establish improved learning."
Name the remaining evaluation
"The outcome review still needs to be carried out."
Protect the review date
"The internal submission is due October 15."
Record unresolved items
"The report should show the gap and proposed next step."
Limit the approval claim
"This update is not an accreditation decision."
Identify authority
"Only the relevant decision body can grant that status."
Use a precise close
"The plan is partly complete, with two actions still open."
Plan follow-up
"Set a follow-up review for unresolved actions and outcomes."
6 language and precision notes
Complete
Requires the specified completion evidence in this case.
Underway
Started; not a synonym for closed.
Unassigned
No accountable owner has yet been designated.
Evidence
Must support the particular claim being made.
Effective
Concerns results, not merely the existence of a process.
Accredited
A status determined through the applicable external process.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which headline accurately reports progress?

Answer and explanation

D. "Three actions complete; one underway; one unassigned."

The correct statement preserves all three status categories supplied in the brief.

2. What should the director decide about Action 5?

Answer and explanation

D. Assign an owner and deadline, without falsely marking it complete.

The missing ownership and timing need a decision, while completion remains unsupported.

3. Which evidence would address effectiveness rather than mere completion?

Answer and explanation

B. A suitable evaluation of whether the actions improved the intended outcomes

Effectiveness concerns the intended result, which requires appropriate outcome evidence rather than presentation or scheduling.

4. What does the October 15 date represent?

Answer and explanation

B. The internal review submission deadline

The brief assigns October 15 to internal submission, not external recognition or outcome proof.

Check explanations in the book, page 97 ↗

E Conversations

Three completed actions are not a closed review

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Amara: Quality coordinator / Hugh: Program director

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • decision authority
  • status report
  • underway
  • action owner
  • follow-up review
  • evidence register
  • effectiveness
  • outcome measure
  • completion evidence
  • evidence gap
  1. Hugh
    The review is due on October fifteenth. I would like a concise progress statement, but the current slide says nearly complete without explaining the remaining work.
  2. Amara
    Use the 1 categories. Three actions have approved completion evidence, Action Four is underway, and Action Five is unassigned. That is a clearer account than nearly complete.
  3. Hugh
    Someone counted Action Four as complete because work has started. That makes the slide show eighty percent.
  4. Amara
    The correct 2 exists for three actions only. Three out of five is sixty percent. Counting the fourth would overstate progress before the action meets its closure requirements.
  5. Hugh
    Action Four has an October tenth deadline and an assigned owner. We can report that schedule without saying the work is already finished.
  6. Amara
    Yes, describe it as 3. Keep its owner and deadline visible. A planned date gives readers useful context, but it does not become a completion date simply because it is approaching.
  7. Hugh
    The fifth action has no owner at all. I do not want that lost in the notes, because nobody is currently accountable for moving it forward.
  8. Amara
    We need an 4 and an agreed deadline. The report should request that decision explicitly. Once assigned, the action remains open until the required work and evidence are completed.
  9. Hugh
    For the three closed actions, where should we point reviewers? The narrative says they are complete, but the supporting documents are in several different places.
  10. Amara
    Link them through the 5. Each claim should lead to the relevant approved record. A persuasive summary still needs a traceable basis, not just a confident description.
  11. Hugh
    One colleague wants to say these completed actions have improved student learning. The work is real, but I have not seen an outcome evaluation yet.
  12. Amara
    Then 6 is not established. We can say the actions were completed, while stating that outcome evaluation remains outstanding. Doing the work and showing its effect are different claims.
  13. Hugh
    We can count the revised documents now. Would that belong in the completion column rather than the learning-results column?
  14. Amara
    That count describes an output, not the intended 7. We need suitable evidence about the learning result. A convenient administrative count should not quietly replace the purpose of the improvement.
  15. Hugh
    The internal submission will therefore show sixty percent complete, the October tenth action still underway, and the unassigned action needing a decision.
  16. Amara
    Include the remaining 8 about outcomes too. Reviewers need to distinguish an incomplete action from an action completed without an evaluated result. Those require different follow-up work.
  17. Hugh
    There is another problem on the cover: accreditation achieved. Can we change that to internal action-plan update before this goes out?
  18. Amara
    Remove that claim. The relevant 9 determines accreditation status through its process. Our action-plan update cannot confer an external status or predict the outcome of a future review.
  19. Hugh
    I will request an owner and date for Action Five today, keep the existing deadline for Action Four, and make the evidence links explicit.
  20. Amara
    Then schedule a 10. The October fifteenth submission should honestly show what is verified, what remains open, and which decisions are needed next, without overstating completion or results.

Answers and explanations
  1. status report Turn 2. The status report should distinguish completed, underway, and unassigned work rather than hide the differences behind a vague label.
  2. completion evidence Turn 4. Completion evidence is required before an action is counted as finished; having an owner and starting work are insufficient.
  3. underway Turn 6. Underway means started but not complete, preserving the distinction between progress and verified closure.
  4. action owner Turn 8. The action owner supplies accountability for progressing the task; assigning one does not itself complete the action.
  5. evidence register Turn 10. The evidence register connects each completion claim with its supporting record so reviewers can verify the stated status.
  6. effectiveness Turn 12. Effectiveness concerns whether intended results were achieved, which has not been established by the supplied completion evidence.
  7. outcome measure Turn 14. The outcome measure should address the result sought, rather than substitute a convenient count of revised documents.
  8. evidence gap Turn 16. The evidence gap concerns missing support for outcome effectiveness as well as the unfinished action, and should remain visible.
  9. decision authority Turn 18. The decision authority for accreditation is distinct from the team preparing an internal progress report.
  10. follow-up review Turn 20. The follow-up review checks unresolved actions and outcome evidence rather than treating submission of the current report as completion of all work.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Hugh: The review is due on October fifteenth. I would like a concise progress statement, but the current slide says nearly complete without explaining the remaining work.
  2. Amara: Use the status report categories. Three actions have approved completion evidence, Action Four is underway, and Action Five is unassigned. That is a clearer account than nearly complete.
  3. Hugh: Someone counted Action Four as complete because work has started. That makes the slide show eighty percent.
  4. Amara: The correct completion evidence exists for three actions only. Three out of five is sixty percent. Counting the fourth would overstate progress before the action meets its closure requirements.
  5. Hugh: Action Four has an October tenth deadline and an assigned owner. We can report that schedule without saying the work is already finished.
  6. Amara: Yes, describe it as underway. Keep its owner and deadline visible. A planned date gives readers useful context, but it does not become a completion date simply because it is approaching.
  7. Hugh: The fifth action has no owner at all. I do not want that lost in the notes, because nobody is currently accountable for moving it forward.
  8. Amara: We need an action owner and an agreed deadline. The report should request that decision explicitly. Once assigned, the action remains open until the required work and evidence are completed.
  9. Hugh: For the three closed actions, where should we point reviewers? The narrative says they are complete, but the supporting documents are in several different places.
  10. Amara: Link them through the evidence register. Each claim should lead to the relevant approved record. A persuasive summary still needs a traceable basis, not just a confident description.
  11. Hugh: One colleague wants to say these completed actions have improved student learning. The work is real, but I have not seen an outcome evaluation yet.
  12. Amara: Then effectiveness is not established. We can say the actions were completed, while stating that outcome evaluation remains outstanding. Doing the work and showing its effect are different claims.
  13. Hugh: We can count the revised documents now. Would that belong in the completion column rather than the learning-results column?
  14. Amara: That count describes an output, not the intended outcome measure. We need suitable evidence about the learning result. A convenient administrative count should not quietly replace the purpose of the improvement.
  15. Hugh: The internal submission will therefore show sixty percent complete, the October tenth action still underway, and the unassigned action needing a decision.
  16. Amara: Include the remaining evidence gap about outcomes too. Reviewers need to distinguish an incomplete action from an action completed without an evaluated result. Those require different follow-up work.
  17. Hugh: There is another problem on the cover: accreditation achieved. Can we change that to internal action-plan update before this goes out?
  18. Amara: Remove that claim. The relevant decision authority determines accreditation status through its process. Our action-plan update cannot confer an external status or predict the outcome of a future review.
  19. Hugh: I will request an owner and date for Action Five today, keep the existing deadline for Action Four, and make the evidence links explicit.
  20. Amara: Then schedule a follow-up review. The October fifteenth submission should honestly show what is verified, what remains open, and which decisions are needed next, without overstating completion or results.
Dialogue in the book, page 64 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Three complete, one underway, one without an owner

Fictional program review using the lesson case: an action plan has five actions; three are complete, one is underway, and one has no owner.

  1. Program director: How should we summarize the five-action plan for the program-review meeting without overstating progress?
  2. Accreditation coordinator: Three actions are complete, one is underway, and one still has no assigned owner.
  3. Program director: Could we describe the plan as substantially closed because most actions are finished?
  4. Accreditation coordinator: That wording could hide the unowned action. State the actual categories and explain the remaining accountability gap.
  5. Program director: Do we have authority to assign the missing owner in this meeting, or must someone else decide?
  6. Accreditation coordinator: That needs confirmation. We should ask the responsible lead rather than put a name in without agreement.
  7. Program director: Please link the completed actions to their evidence so the review can verify the progress.
  8. Accreditation coordinator: I will, while keeping the underway item separate from work merely planned.
  9. Program director: Our decision ask will be to confirm ownership and the next checkpoint for the unfinished work.
  10. Accreditation coordinator: Good. That provides a useful status without implying the whole action plan or accreditation process is complete.
Evidence uploaded, review still pending

Fictional accreditation preparation: a department uploaded evidence files, but the review team has not assessed their coverage or relevance.

  1. Department administrator: The evidence files are uploaded. Can we mark the accreditation evidence requirement as satisfied?
  2. Review coordinator: Upload is complete, but the reviewers haven't assessed relevance or coverage against the requested criteria.
  3. Department administrator: Then accessible files and accepted evidence are different milestones in this preparation process.
  4. Review coordinator: Correct. We should report the submission and keep the assessment outcome pending.
  5. Department administrator: Should I assume the reviewers agree if they don't comment before the next update?
  6. Review coordinator: No. Silence isn't confirmation of review or acceptance. Request a status through the agreed coordination route.
  7. Department administrator: I'll identify the submitted file versions and ask whether any access or evidence gaps have been found.
  8. Review coordinator: Good. Don't replace files silently while the review is underway; preserve clear version references.
  9. Department administrator: The update will say submission complete, coverage assessment pending, and reviewer response requested.
  10. Review coordinator: That gives the program team accurate progress without turning document availability into a completed accreditation judgment.
An action was performed, its effect is unassessed

Fictional program-review action: a revised orientation session was delivered, but the planned learner-feedback review has not occurred.

  1. Program lead: We delivered the revised orientation. Can we say the action has improved learner understanding?
  2. Quality coordinator: Delivery is complete, but the planned feedback review hasn't occurred, so the effect remains unassessed.
  3. Program lead: Then the action plan should distinguish implementation from the evidence of its intended benefit.
  4. Quality coordinator: Exactly. A completed session doesn't establish how learners understood or used the information.
  5. Program lead: Could we still recognize the staff's completed work without claiming an outcome we haven't checked?
  6. Quality coordinator: Yes. Report the session delivered and identify the feedback review as the next pending step.
  7. Program lead: I'll ask the evaluation owner to confirm when the available feedback can be reviewed.
  8. Quality coordinator: Please avoid inventing a response rate or positive result before the evidence is examined.
  9. Program lead: The program-review update will retain both the completed activity and the open effectiveness question.
  10. Quality coordinator: Good. That makes the improvement process traceable and keeps the evidence claim within what is actually known.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

Three completed actions are not a closed review
  1. Read turns 1-8, stressing sixty percent and the distinction between underway and complete.
  2. Repeat turns 11-18, separating outputs, outcomes, and accreditation status.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Count only verified completion

A fictional plan has eight actions: five have approved completion evidence, two are underway, and one is unassigned. The internal report is due Friday. No external accreditation decision has been issued.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Coordinator: "The completed-action count is ___."

Answer and explanation

A. five

Only the five actions with approved completion evidence are counted as complete.

2. Director: "The completion rate is ___ percent."

Answer and explanation

A. 62.5

Five divided by eight equals sixty-two point five percent.

3. Coordinator: "The action without an owner is ___."

Answer and explanation

D. unassigned

Unassigned describes the missing accountability, not completed or underway work.

4. Director: "External accreditation is ___."

Answer and explanation

B. not confirmed

The brief states that no external accreditation decision has been issued.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Build vocabulary and collocations — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

Lesson 07: Accreditation and Program Review
Original fictional case: Quality coordinator Amara and program director Hugh prepare a fictional internal review due on October 15. Its action plan has five actions. Actions 1, 2, and 3 have approved completion evidence. Action 4 is underway, with an assigned owner and an October 10 deadline. Action 5 has no assigned owner and no completion evidence. No outcome evaluation has yet established whether the completed actions improved learning. The team must report three of five actions complete, seek an owner and deadline for Action 5, and avoid presenting the internal review as an accreditation decision.
Language workshop: Three completed actions are not a closed review
Communication goal: Report action status, ownership, and evidence without confusing completion with effectiveness or accreditation approval.
Language guidance: Three completed actions out of five is 60%. Action 4 is underway, not complete, and Action 5 is unassigned. Counting an underway item as completed would inflate the rate to 80% without the required evidence.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): "Three of five actions have approved completion evidence." / "That is 60% complete." / "Action 4 is in progress, not closed." / "Its assigned owner is working toward October 10." / "Action 5 remains unassigned." / "We need an owner and deadline for Action 5." / "Link each claim to its supporting record." / "Do not count underway work as complete." / "Completion does not yet establish improved learning." / "The outcome review still needs to be carried out." / "The internal submission is due October 15." / "The report should show the gap and proposed next step." / "This update is not an accreditation decision." / "Only the relevant decision body can grant that status." / "The plan is partly complete, with two actions still open." / "Set a follow-up review for unresolved actions and outcomes."
Vocabulary: program review: A structured examination of a program's quality and performance. / accreditation: External recognition under a specified quality-assurance process. / self-study: An institution's documented examination of its own performance. / review panel: A group examining a program against relevant expectations. / criterion for review: A standard used to evaluate a program. / supporting evidence: Material used to substantiate a review claim. / evidence register: An index connecting evidence to claims or requirements. / action plan: A set of improvement actions with responsibilities and timing. / action owner: The person accountable for advancing an assigned action. / due date: The date by which an item is expected. / completion evidence: Information showing that an action was carried out. / status report: A summary of current progress and unresolved items. / underway: Started but not complete. / unassigned: Without a designated responsible person. / closure criterion: A condition required before an item is closed. / effectiveness: The extent to which an action achieves its intended result. / outcome measure: An indicator of the result being sought. / implementation evidence: Information showing that a practice was put into use. / continuous improvement: Repeated use of evidence to improve practice. / follow-up review: A later check of progress or results. / evidence gap: Missing support for a claim or required conclusion. / accountability: Clear responsibility for actions and explanations. / review finding: A conclusion reached through examination of evidence. / decision authority: The body or role permitted to make a decision.
Speaking task: Read turns 1-8, stressing sixty percent and the distinction between underway and complete. Repeat turns 11-18, separating outputs, outcomes, and accreditation status. Switch roles for the transfer; read 62.5 percent aloud and check its explanation.
Partner: Amara: Quality coordinator / Hugh: Program director
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A fictional plan has eight actions: five have approved completion evidence, two are underway, and one is unassigned. The internal report is due Friday. No external accreditation decision has been issued.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Hugh: There is another problem on the cover: accreditation achieved. Can we change that to internal action-plan update before this goes out?
Amara: Remove that claim. The relevant decision authority determines accreditation status through its process. Our action-plan update cannot confer an external status or predict the outcome of a future review.
Hugh: I will request an owner and date for Action Five today, keep the existing deadline for Action Four, and make the evidence links explicit.
Amara: Then schedule a follow-up review. The October fifteenth submission should honestly show what is verified, what remains open, and which decisions are needed next, without overstating completion or results.
END REFERENCE
Practice grammar in context — complete prompt

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Hugh: There is another problem on the cover: accreditation achieved. Can we change that to internal action-plan update before this goes out?
Amara: Remove that claim. The relevant decision authority determines accreditation status through its process. Our action-plan update cannot confer an external status or predict the outcome of a future review.
Hugh: I will request an owner and date for Action Five today, keep the existing deadline for Action Four, and make the evidence links explicit.
Amara: Then schedule a follow-up review. The October fifteenth submission should honestly show what is verified, what remains open, and which decisions are needed next, without overstating completion or results.
END REFERENCE
08Budget, Staffing, and Institutional PrioritiesOne more class still leaves four learners waiting+

Your goal

Compare staffing options using capacity, full cost, displaced work, and authorization status.

This lesson in the book, page 67 ↗

A Read the situation

One more class still leaves four learners waiting

Operations manager Kiran and principal Elise consider one extra class for a waiting list of 24 learners. The class cap is 20, so one class would leave four learners waiting. Option A reallocates an existing qualified teacher for four weekly teaching hours, removing four weekly tutoring hours; it has no additional direct staffing charge. Option B uses confirmed available, qualified temporary support at an all-inclusive cost of $4,800 against an uncommitted $5,000 budget, preserving tutoring. Both options meet room and timetable requirements. Funding approval is pending. The stated priority is preserving tutoring while adding capacity.

Kiran: Operations manager / Elise: Principal

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. How many learners would remain waiting after one extra class?

Answer and explanation

B. Four

The waiting list has twenty-four learners, while the extra class has only twenty places.

2. What does Option A displace?

Answer and explanation

C. Four weekly tutoring hours

The existing teacher's four teaching hours replace four weekly tutoring hours.

3. Which option matches the stated priority within the supplied budget?

Answer and explanation

C. Option B, subject to funding approval

Option B preserves tutoring and fits the budget, but approval remains pending.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Option B costs $4,800 against $5,000 available, leaving $200 of budget headroom. The all-inclusive cost and availability are supplied facts. Staying within budget does not itself grant funding approval or authorize a commitment.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
staffing allocation
Assignment of personnel time to work.review staffing allocations
reallocation
Moving an existing resource to another use.propose a staff reallocation
teaching load
The amount of teaching assigned to a staff member.assess teaching load
contact hour
An hour of direct teaching or scheduled learner contact.calculate contact hours
tutoring provision
The organized availability of additional learning support.preserve tutoring provision
temporary appointment
A staffing arrangement for a limited period.approve a temporary appointment
qualification requirement
The credential or competence needed for a role.verify qualification requirements
availability check
Confirmation that a person or resource can be scheduled.complete an availability check
class cap
The maximum permitted class size in the stated setting.respect the class cap
additional capacity
Extra places or service volume available.add teaching capacity
unmet demand
Need or requests not covered by available provision.quantify unmet demand
waiting-list balance
The number still waiting after an allocation.calculate the waiting-list balance
direct cost
A charge directly attributable to an option.compare direct costs
all-inclusive cost
A stated price including all specified costs for the option.confirm the all-inclusive cost
budget headroom
The amount remaining within an available budget.calculate budget headroom
uncommitted budget
Funds not yet assigned to another approved use.verify the uncommitted budget
opportunity cost
The value of the alternative use given up.explain the opportunity cost
service trade-off
A gain in one service accompanied by a loss elsewhere.state the service trade-off
staffing business case
A reasoned proposal for a staffing decision.present a staffing business case
funding approval
Authorization to use funds for a specified purpose.obtain funding approval
timetable feasibility
Whether a proposal fits the available schedule.check timetable feasibility
room availability
Whether a suitable teaching space can be used.confirm room availability
institutional priority
A stated objective guiding organizational choices.align with institutional priorities
decision condition
A requirement that must hold for a decision to proceed.state the decision conditions

Option A has no additional direct staffing charge, but removes four weekly tutoring hours. That service trade-off is not a dollar amount given in the brief. Neither option accommodates all 24 waiting learners in a single 20-place class.

C Notice the language

Show what an option displaces

A proposal with no additional charge can still reduce another service. Name that effect without dismissing budget constraints. A recommendation is more useful when it states the priority served, the remaining capacity gap, and the approval still required.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
State demand
"There are 24 learners on the waiting list."
State the cap
"The extra class can take 20."
Keep unmet demand visible
"Four learners would still be waiting."
Explain reallocation
"Option A moves four weekly hours from tutoring to teaching."
Avoid a misleading zero
"There is no additional charge, but tutoring capacity falls."
State the temporary cost
"Option B has an all-inclusive cost of $4,800."
Identify available funds
"The uncommitted budget is $5,000."
Calculate headroom
"That leaves $200 within the budget."
Confirm supplied readiness
"Qualifications, availability, room, and timetable are confirmed."
Name the priority
"The stated priority is preserving tutoring while adding places."
Make the recommendation
"Option B best meets that priority on the supplied facts."
Keep approval distinct
"Funding approval is still pending."
Avoid premature commitment
"Do not confirm the appointment before authorization."
Explain opportunity cost
"Reallocation gives up four tutoring hours each week."
Bound the claim
"This adds one class; it does not clear the waiting list."
Close with the decision
"We need approval for Option B and a separate response to the remaining demand."
6 language and precision notes
No additional charge
Does not mean no effect on other services.
All-inclusive
Use the specified scope; do not assume hidden exclusions without evidence.
Available budget
Not the same as permission to spend.
Qualified and available
Necessary readiness information, not a signed appointment.
Headroom
Budget remaining after the stated cost.
Recommended
A proposed choice, not an already authorized commitment.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. What is the budget headroom under Option B?

Answer and explanation

C. $200

Subtracting the all-inclusive cost of four thousand eight hundred from five thousand leaves two hundred.

2. Which description of Option A is most accurate?

Answer and explanation

D. No additional direct charge, but four weekly tutoring hours are displaced.

The option's financial charge is zero while its supplied service trade-off is real.

3. Which recommendation is best supported?

Answer and explanation

D. Recommend Option B to preserve tutoring, subject to approval, while acknowledging four learners still waiting.

This recommendation follows the stated priority, budget, approval boundary, and remaining capacity gap.

4. What is still required before a staffing commitment?

Answer and explanation

B. Funding approval

The brief confirms operational readiness but explicitly leaves funding authorization pending.

Check explanations in the book, page 99 ↗

E Conversations

One more class still leaves four learners waiting

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Kiran: Operations manager / Elise: Principal

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • service trade-off
  • class cap
  • unmet demand
  • reallocation
  • all-inclusive cost
  • budget headroom
  • Funding approval
  • decision conditions
  • staffing business case
  • institutional priority
  1. Elise
    Twenty-four learners are waiting. The proposal for one additional class needs to explain staffing and remaining demand clearly.
  2. Kiran
    The 1 is twenty. One class adds twenty places and leaves four learners waiting. Neither staffing option changes that limit, so we should not describe either as clearing the list.
  3. Elise
    Option A uses an existing qualified teacher for four hours each week. There is no additional direct staffing charge, which makes it look attractive.
  4. Kiran
    But the 2 removes four weekly tutoring hours. The teacher's time is not an unused resource. The proposal needs to show what is displaced, not just the absence of a new invoice.
  5. Elise
    Our priority is preserving tutoring while adding places. What is the full cost of temporary support?
  6. Kiran
    Its 3 is four thousand eight hundred dollars. The person is qualified and available, and both options meet the room and timetable requirements. Those readiness checks are confirmed.
  7. Elise
    We have five thousand uncommitted dollars. Please state the remaining amount, rather than saying the cost is approximately within budget.
  8. Kiran
    The 4 is two hundred dollars. Five thousand minus four thousand eight hundred leaves that amount. The option fits the supplied budget, although fitting it does not authorize spending.
  9. Elise
    Good. Funding approval is still pending. We should not tell the temporary teacher that the appointment is confirmed while the decision is unresolved.
  10. Kiran
    Exactly. 5 remains a decision condition. We can recommend the option and describe readiness, but we must keep the appointment unconfirmed until the authorized decision is made.
  11. Elise
    Let us keep no additional staffing charge in the comparison, but put the lost tutoring hours right beside it. That is the actual choice.
  12. Kiran
    State the 6 in its own terms: four tutoring hours are lost each week. The brief does not price that loss. We should neither ignore it nor invent a dollar equivalent.
  13. Elise
    Then Option B meets the tutoring priority more closely, while Option A reduces that service. Both add the same twenty places and fit the practical timetable.
  14. Kiran
    That supports the 7 for Option B. The recommendation follows the supplied priority and facts, not a general claim that temporary staffing is always better than using existing staff.
  15. Elise
    Before families hear about an extra class, the message needs to say twenty places, not twenty-four. What happens to the remaining four is still unresolved.
  16. Kiran
    Keep that 8 visible. The proposal adds capacity without resolving every request. Any further provision would need its own feasible plan and authorization rather than being implied by this decision.
  17. Elise
    The final paragraph should ask for the exact approval needed, state the full cost, and make clear that tutoring is preserved under the recommended option.
  18. Kiran
    I will connect it to the 9 and record the two hundred dollars remaining. We should also state the capacity limit so the budget decision is not confused with a promise to all families.
  19. Elise
    Please request approval. Do not confirm the appointment or change tutoring while the decision is pending.
  20. Kiran
    I will state those 10 explicitly. The recommended class can add twenty places and preserve tutoring for four thousand eight hundred dollars, subject to approval, with four learners still waiting.

Answers and explanations
  1. class cap Turn 2. The class cap limits the new class to twenty learners, so it cannot accommodate the entire twenty-four-person waiting list.
  2. reallocation Turn 4. Reallocation moves existing staff time from tutoring to the new class, creating a service loss as well as extra teaching capacity.
  3. all-inclusive cost Turn 6. The supplied all-inclusive cost is four thousand eight hundred dollars, not an hourly rate or an incomplete estimate.
  4. budget headroom Turn 8. Budget headroom is the two hundred dollars remaining after subtracting the stated cost from the uncommitted budget.
  5. Funding approval Turn 10. Funding approval is the authorization still needed before a commitment, separate from available funds and operational readiness.
  6. service trade-off Turn 12. The service trade-off is the loss of four weekly tutoring hours; the brief does not assign that loss a dollar value.
  7. staffing business case Turn 14. The staffing business case connects the recommendation to cost, capacity, feasibility, and the stated institutional priority.
  8. unmet demand Turn 16. Unmet demand is the remaining need not covered by the new class, here four learners still awaiting places.
  9. institutional priority Turn 18. The institutional priority is the supplied objective of preserving tutoring while adding capacity, which justifies the choice between otherwise feasible options.
  10. decision conditions Turn 20. Decision conditions define what must be authorized before the proposal proceeds, preventing a recommendation from being treated as a completed commitment.
Read the complete conversation

  9. Elise: Good. Funding approval is still pending. We should not tell the temporary teacher that the appointment is confirmed while the decision is unresolved.
